  the goal of the experiment is for writing a paper regarding "Explainable AI:
  LIME Cost/Quality Scaling on High-Dimensional Tabular Data across multiple
  different models ".

  read the log_entries for more information.

  Forest. we will exclude TabFM for now.
  we have a file called controlled_lime_protocol.py as an attempt to
  standardise the tests between the different models, so there are as little
  different variables between them, making the comparisons more accurate.

  the current problem is that it is very messy at the moment. we are currently
  running the experiments in the experiments folder. most of them don't
  actually store the actual results at the end. some are still using old
  methods that don't align with the methodology anymore. there is also a lot of
  fluff in the jupiter notebooks for visualisation, and other things that may
  not be up to date.

  there is a lot of information regarding the current experiments and how they
  contrast with the current methodologies in the "notes" folder.

  i want you to make a plan to re-do the whole thing, from organising the
  files, to re-coding the experiments again to ensure they align with each
  other, standardising any variable between them that we can.

  we keep all the standardised methods, values, parameters between each
  model/experiment, uses the most up to date methodology and each will store
  their results in a results folder. I then want a separate script that will

     │   Jaccard on the y axis, with one line per model, marker size or shape  │
     │   for feature count, and points ordered by budget. This directly        │
     │   answers "where does extra compute stop improving quality".            │

     │ - A predictive-performance table (figures/performance_table.csv) built  │

     │   both PNG and PDF.                                                     │

     │                                                                         │
     │ 1. Create lime_study/config.py and protocol.py (porting from            │
     │    experiments/controlled_lime_protocol.py).                            │
     │ 2. Add the three model scripts.                                         │
     │ 3. Smoke test: add a --quick flag to run_all.py / run_model_experiment  │
     │    that overrides the sweep to 1 case per class, budgets (500, 1000),   │
     │    and 2 repeats, and writes to results/quick/. Use it to check the     │
     │    whole pipeline in under a minute.                                    │
     │ 4. Add plot_results.py, then run_all.py.                                │
     │ 5. Add the README and the pinned requirements. Note that the root       │
     │    requirements.txt is gitignored, so the study gets its own file.      │
     │ 6. Run the full study. Expect roughly 4,725 explanations; Random Forest │
     │    at 10k samples will be the slowest.                                  │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Verification                                                            │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - python -m lime_study.run_all --quick finishes, writes every file type │
     │   to results/quick/, and produces both figures.                         │
     │ - On the full run, the built-in assertions pass: 1,575 raw rows per     │
     │   model, 15 summary rows per model, 45 combined, no unexpected NaNs in  │
     │   the stability means.                                                  │
     │ - Sanity checks:                                                        │
     │   - runtime grows roughly linearly with num_samples;                    │
     │   - Jaccard is non-decreasing with budget;                              │
     │   - predictive accuracy for each model is in a plausible range (RF/XGB  │
     │     ~0.8+, LR lower);                                                   │
     │   - run_meta.json shows lime 0.2.0.1 and n_jobs=1.                      │
     │ - Run one model twice: fidelity and stability values should be          │
     │   identical (deterministic seeds), and only runtimes should differ.     │

⏺ User approved Claude's plan
  ⎿  Plan saved to: ~/.claude/plans/i-need-help-planning-merry-island.md · /plan
     to edit
     Plan: Rebuild the controlled LIME cost/quality experiment

     Context

     Paper: "Explainable AI: LIME Cost/Quality Scaling on High-Dimensional
     Tabular Data across multiple models". It measures Runtime, Fidelity
     (explanation.score) and Stability (top-5 Jaccard + Spearman) across
     perturbed-feature counts 18/36/54 and LIME budgets 500–10,000, for XGBoost,
     Logistic Regression and Random Forest (TabFM is left out for now).

     The current experiments/ notebooks are messy. They mix outdated exploratory
     cells (one-row runs, top-5/5 overlap instead of Jaccard,
     explainer-construction timing, full-data splits) with a final
     benchmark_lime() call that has never saved results.
     notes/methodology_comparison.md and notes/experiment_handoff.md list the
     gaps: LIME settings are implicit, Logistic Regression has no random_state,
     only XGBoost uses n_jobs=4, stability rows are thrown away, and versions
     aren't pinned or recorded.

     The goal is a new, self-contained folder with:
     - one config module that holds every shared value;
     - one shared protocol module;
     - one thin script per model, each writing results to results/;
     - one runner that executes all three and produces the final figure.

     The old folders are not touched.

     Decisions (agreed)

     - Data: stratified 10,000-row sample, then a stratified 80/20 split
       (8k/2k), seed 42. LIME background is 2,000 rows. The methodology section
       must say this explicitly.
     - Format: plain .py scripts. Old experiments/ and coverTree_activities/
       stay where they are.
     - Fairness: single-threaded inference for every model (n_jobs=1, with
       thread env vars pinned) and random_state=42 on every model.
     - Not in scope: stage-level timing and local_pred agreement. Runtime is
       total explain_instance() wall-clock time.
     - Dataset: Covertype only, but all dataset-specific code sits in one block
       of config.py so a new dataset can be added later.
     - Final output: a 3×3 grid (rows: runtime / fidelity / Jaccard; columns: 18
       / 36 / 54 features; x axis: num_samples on a log scale; one line per
       model with ±std bands), plus a cost-vs-quality plot (runtime vs Jaccard).

     New layout: lime_study/

     lime_study/
       README.md               # how to run, what each file does, methodology
     summary
       requirements.txt        # pinned: lime==0.2.0.1, scikit-learn==1.7.2,
     xgboost==3.2.0, numpy, pandas, scipy, matplotlib
       config.py               # ALL shared constants + model definitions
       protocol.py             # data loading, case selection, feature subsets,
     LIME benchmark, metrics, saving
       models/
         __init__.py
         run_xgboost.py
         run_logistic_regression.py
         run_random_forest.py
       run_all.py              # runs the 3 models (or --models subset), then
     calls plot_results
       plot_results.py         # reads results/*.csv → figures/
       results/                # <model>_raw.csv, <model>_stability.csv,
     <model>_summary.csv, <model>_performance.json, <model>_run_meta.json
       figures/                # lime_scaling_grid.png/.pdf,
     cost_vs_quality.png/.pdf, combined_summary.csv

     config.py: the single source of truth

     - Seeds/data: RANDOM_STATE=42, SAMPLE_SIZE=10_000, TEST_SIZE=0.20,
       BACKGROUND_SIZE=2_000.
     - Dataset block: DATASET_NAME="covertype", N_NUMERIC=10, CLASS_NAMES,
       FEATURE_COUNTS=(18,36,54).
     - Sweep: SAMPLE_BUDGETS=(500,1000,2500,5000,10000), REPEATS=5,
       EXPLANATIONS_PER_CLASS=3, NUM_FEATURES=10, TOP_K=5.
     - Explicit LIME settings (LIME_EXPLAINER_KWARGS):
       discretize_continuous=True, discretizer="quartile", kernel_width=None
       (documented as 0.75·sqrt(d)), feature_selection="auto",
       mode="classification".
     - Explicit explain_instance kwargs: model_regressor=Ridge(alpha=1,
       fit_intercept=True, random_state=...), which matches LIME's default but
       is now stated explicitly.
     - Threading: N_JOBS=1. set_single_thread_env() sets OMP_NUM_THREADS,
       OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS, MKL_NUM_THREADS and VECLIB_MAXIMUM_THREADS (macOS
       Accelerate) to 1 before numpy is imported.
     - Model factories (build_xgboost(), build_logistic_regression(),
       build_random_forest()): these keep the existing hyperparameters from the
       handoff table and change only n_jobs=1 and random_state=42:
       - XGB: n_estimators=250, max_depth=6, learning_rate=0.1, subsample=0.9,
         colsample_bytree=0.9, objective="multi:softprob",
         eval_metric="mlogloss", tree_method="hist"
       - LR: a Pipeline with a ColumnTransformer that applies StandardScaler to
         the first 10 columns and passes the rest through, then
         LogisticRegression(max_iter=1000, solver="lbfgs")
       - RF: RandomForestClassifier(n_estimators=100)
     - MODEL_NAMES = ("XGBoost", "LogisticRegression", "RandomForest") gives
       consistent labels and output filenames.
     - Paths: RESULTS_DIR and FIGURES_DIR, resolved relative to the package.

     protocol.py: ported from experiments/controlled_lime_protocol.py

     Copy and clean up the existing logic so its behaviour stays identical, and
     read constants from config:
     - load_controlled_covtype(), selected_explanation_cases(), feature_subset()
       and evaluate_predictive_performance() stay unchanged apart from reading
       config.
     - make_predict_proba(model, feature_names) is a shared DataFrame wrapper,
       so every model gets column names the same way. This replaces the ad-hoc
       wrappers in each notebook.
     - benchmark_lime(model, X_train, X_test, y_test, model_name):
       - uses the same loop as now, but passes **LIME_EXPLAINER_KWARGS and
         model_regressor explicitly;
       - returns stability as a third DataFrame (currently it is thrown away);
       - prints simple progress (case i/21);
       - adds a fit_seconds column to the model metadata (not mixed into the
         LIME runtime).
     - summarise(raw, stability): the existing aggregation, plus
       fidelity_median, spearman_std, jaccard_std and NaN counts.
     - run_model_experiment(model_name, build_fn): the shared driver every model
       script calls. It:
       a. loads data;
       b. fits and times the model;
       c. computes performance;
       d. runs the benchmark;
       e. calls save_results(), which writes the raw, stability and summary
          CSVs, the performance JSON, and the run-meta JSON (package versions,
          Python version, platform/CPU, timestamp, config snapshot, model
          get_params());
       f. checks the expected counts: 1,575 raw rows, 3,150 stability rows, 15
          summary rows, and 21 cases / 105 runs per group.

     models/run_*.py

     Each script has about 10 lines: set_single_thread_env(), then
     run_model_experiment("XGBoost", config.build_xgboost) under if __name__ ==
     "__main__". Model-specific code lives only in the build_* factory, so there
     is nothing else to drift.

     run_all.py

     Runs python -m lime_study.run_all [--models XGBoost RandomForest]
     [--skip-plots] [--plots-only].
     - Each model runs in its own subprocess, so thread env vars and the warm-up
       are fresh and one model can't slow another's timings.
     - Models run in sequence, never in parallel, because parallel runs would
       distort runtime.
     - When all models finish, it calls plot_results.main().

     plot_results.py

     - Concatenates results/*_summary.csv into figures/combined_summary.csv. The
       expected size is 45 rows.
     - Figure 1 (lime_scaling_grid): a 3×3 grid as agreed. The y axis is shared
       within each row. Runtime uses mean ±std. Fidelity is labelled "Local
       surrogate fit (R²)". Stability is top-5 Jaccard.
     - Figure 2 (cost_vs_quality): mean runtime on the x axis (log) against
       Jaccard on the y axis, with one line per model, marker size or shape for
       feature count, and points ordered by budget. This directly answers "where
       does extra compute stop improving quality".
     - A secondary Spearman version of the stability row, saved as
       stability_spearman.png.
     - A predictive-performance table (figures/performance_table.csv) built from
       the JSON files.
     - Uses matplotlib only, at a size suited to an IEEE column, and saves both
       PNG and PDF.

     Implementation order

     0. Document the plan in the repo: write notes/lime_study_rebuild_plan.md so
        the rest of the team can read it (especially Amelia, who is writing the
        methodology, and Hugh, who is writing the results). It covers the
        context, the agreed decisions, the folder layout, what each file is
        responsible for, the standardised config values, the run instructions
        and the verification checks. It sits next to the existing
        notes/methodology_comparison.md and notes/experiment_handoff.md. It ends
        with a "Changes from the old experiments" section: explicit LIME
        kwargs, n_jobs=1 for all models, random_state=42 for LR, stability rows
        saved, run metadata recorded. Keep this file updated if anything changes
        during implementation.
     1. Create lime_study/config.py and protocol.py (porting from
        experiments/controlled_lime_protocol.py).
     2. Add the three model scripts.
     3. Smoke test: add a --quick flag to run_all.py / run_model_experiment that
        overrides the sweep to 1 case per class, budgets (500, 1000), and 2
        repeats, and writes to results/quick/. Use it to check the whole
        pipeline in under a minute.
     4. Add plot_results.py, then run_all.py.
     5. Add the README and the pinned requirements. Note that the root
        requirements.txt is gitignored, so the study gets its own file.
     6. Run the full study. Expect roughly 4,725 explanations; Random Forest at
        10k samples will be the slowest.

     Verification

     - python -m lime_study.run_all --quick finishes, writes every file type to
       results/quick/, and produces both figures.
     - On the full run, the built-in assertions pass: 1,575 raw rows per model,
       15 summary rows per model, 45 combined, no unexpected NaNs in the
       stability means.
     - Sanity checks:
       - runtime grows roughly linearly with num_samples;
       - Jaccard is non-decreasing with budget;
       - predictive accuracy for each model is in a plausible range (RF/XGB
         ~0.8+, LR lower);
       - run_meta.json shows lime 0.2.0.1 and n_jobs=1.
     - Run one model twice: fidelity and stability values should be identical
       (deterministic seeds), and only runtimes should differ.

  Read 2 files (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I wrote the plan up for the team in notes/lime_study_rebuild_plan.md, built
  the new lime_study/ folder, and ran the full study. All three models finished
  in about 4 minutes in total, and every built-in check passed.

  Documentation
  - notes/lime_study_rebuild_plan.md covers why we rebuilt, the agreed
    decisions, the folder layout, the exact settings for each model, how to run
    it, the checks, a "Changes from the old experiments" list (useful for
    Amelia's methodology section) and the limitations to state in the paper.
  - lime_study/README.md is a shorter how-to-run guide.

  Code (old experiments/ and coverTree_activities/ are untouched)
  - config.py (lime_study/config.py) holds every shared value: seeds, split, the
    2,000-row background, 18/36/54 features, budgets, repeats, the LIME
    settings (now set explicitly), and the three model definitions. Every model
    now has n_jobs=1 and random_state=42.
  - protocol.py (lime_study/protocol.py) contains the shared benchmark logic,
    ported from controlled_lime_protocol.py with the same sampling and metrics.
    It now also saves the stability rows, the feature weights from each
    explanation, and run metadata (versions, machine, settings). It checks the
    expected row counts before saving.
  - models/run_*.py (lime_study/models/) are three-line scripts that only name
    their model, so the models can't drift apart.
  - run_all.py (lime_study/run_all.py) runs each model in a fresh process, one
    after another, then calls plot_results.py (lime_study/plot_results.py),
    which draws the figures.

  Run results
  - 1,575 explanations and 3,150 stability pairs per model, 45 summary rows in
    total, and no NaNs.
  - Re-running a model gives identical fidelity, weights and stability; only the
    runtimes change.
  - Test accuracy: Random Forest 0.807, XGBoost 0.798, Logistic Regression
    0.721.
  - Figures are in lime_study/figures/: lime_scaling_grid, cost_vs_quality and
    stability_spearman, each as PNG and PDF. combined_summary.csv and
    performance_table.csv are there too.

  What the figures show
  - Runtime grows roughly in line with the sample budget.
  - Fidelity stays flat as the budget grows but falls as the feature count goes
    up (except for Random Forest).
  - Stability improves with budget for every model. Random Forest is the most
    stable, and Logistic Regression and XGBoost become much less stable at 36
    and 54 features.

  Things to know
  - Logistic Regression at 36 features and 1,000 samples has one runtime spike,
    which widens its error band. It is probably background load on the machine.
    For the paper's final numbers, re-run on an idle machine (python -m
    lime_study.run_all --models LogisticRegression) or report medians, which are
    already in the summary CSV.
  - Plotting first failed because your matplotlib font cache is stale. I didn't
    touch your cache and ran the plots with a temporary one instead. Delete
    ~/.matplotlib/fontlist-*.json once and it will rebuild itself.
  - The pinned package list is called requirements-pinned.txt because the repo's
    .gitignore ignores every file named requirements.txt.
  - I added __pycache__/ and the smoke-test output folders to .gitignore. That
    file also ignores itself, so those entries stay on your machine only.

  Nothing is committed yet. The results and figures CSVs are small enough to
  commit alongside the code if you want them in the repo.

⏺ I re-ran Logistic Regression. The spike at 36 features / 1,000 samples is
  gone: the slowest explanation there dropped from 0.070s to 0.011s and the std
  from 0.0064 to 0.0010. But new spikes showed up elsewhere in the same run:

  ┌────────────────────┬─────────┬────────┬─────────┬──────────────────┐
  │ Features / samples │  Mean   │  Std   │ Slowest │ Typical (median) │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────┼────────┼─────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ 18 / 1,000         │ 0.0085s │ 0.0114 │ 0.071s  │ ~0.006s          │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────┼────────┼─────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ 18 / 2,500         │ 0.0116s │ 0.0131 │ 0.122s  │ ~0.010s          │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────┼────────┼─────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ 54 / 2,500         │ 0.0117s │ 0.0075 │ 0.084s  │ ~0.011s          │
  └────────────────────┴─────────┴────────┴─────────┴──────────────────┘

  These are one-off hiccups where a single explanation takes 10–20× longer than
  usual, most likely the operating system briefly pausing the process. Logistic
  Regression is hit hardest because its explanations take only about 5ms, so one
  100ms pause noticeably shifts a mean over 105 runs. Re-running again would
  just move the spikes around, so another re-run won't produce clean numbers.

  There are two reliable fixes:
  1. Plot the median runtime with an interquartile band instead of mean ± std
     (recommended). Spikes like these barely move it. It's a small change to
     protocol.py (adding the quartiles to the summary) and the runtime row in
     plot_results.py, and needs no re-run. The paper would say "median runtime
     (IQR)".
  2. Time each explanation 3 times and keep the fastest. This is standard for
     benchmarks, but it triples the run time to about 12 minutes, and the
     methodology would need to describe it.

  The new Logistic Regression results and the updated figures are already saved
  in lime_study/results/ and lime_study/figures/, so they reflect this latest
  run. Which fix do you want?

❯ how do i run it myself?

⏺ From the repo root (the XAI folder) in a terminal:

  cd "/Users/ExtraAccount/Documents/college
  subjects/block_10/xAI/xAI_Project/XAI"
  source .venv/bin/activate

  The venv already has the right package versions. On a new machine you'd first
  run pip install -r lime_study/requirements-pinned.txt.

  One-time fix: your matplotlib font cache is out of date and will make plotting
  crash. Delete it once and matplotlib will rebuild it:
  rm ~/.matplotlib/fontlist-*.json

  Commands:
  python -m lime_study.run_all --quick                       # ~20 s smoke test,
  writes to results/quick + figures/quick
  python -m lime_study.run_all                               # full study, all 3
  models (~4–5 min)
  python -m lime_study.run_all --models LogisticRegression   # just one model
  (or several, space-separated)
  python -m lime_study.run_all --plots-only                  # redraw figures
  from saved results, no re-run
  python -m lime_study.models.run_random_forest              # one model,
  without plotting

  Model names for --models are XGBoost, LogisticRegression and RandomForest.

  A few things to know:
  - Use python -m ... from the repo root. Running python lime_study/run_all.py
    directly breaks the imports.
  - Keep the machine idle while it runs. Runtime is one of the measurements, so
    close browsers, Spotify and so on.
  - Each run overwrites that model's files in lime_study/results/, then run_all
    redraws lime_study/figures/.
  - You'll see progress lines like [XGBoost] case 5/21 done. If a run produces
    the wrong number of rows, it stops with an AssertionError instead of saving.

  You can also run it from VS Code's integrated terminal. Just make sure the
  .venv interpreter is the one selected.

  561 numeric input features, 10,299 rows, six classes, and no missing values.
  UCI documents a subject-based split, with volunteers separated between
  training and test sets. For the dimensionality sweep, 100, 300, and 500
  features would stay within TabFM’s documented 500-feature limit and preserve
  the same conditions across models.

⏺ The research checks out. Before I write the plan, a few decisions:

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Which HAR source and train/test split should we use? Only the original
     UCI download includes the subject IDs. The OpenML copy (ID 1478) leaves
     them out, so a subject-based split isn't possible from OpenML. → UCI
     original split (Recommended)
     · What should happen to Covertype? → Keep both via --dataset (Recommended)
     · What should the perturbed-feature sweep be? All models always predict on
     the full 561 features. Only LIME's perturbed subset changes. → 100 / 300 /
     500 (Recommended)
     · There's still the earlier runtime-spike question. How should runtime be
     reported? → Keep mean ± std

     │                                                                         │
     │ Alternatives considered (verified) and why we rejected them:            │
     │ - Isolet (UCI 54): 7,797 × 617, 26 classes. Too many classes for 3      │
     │   cases per class.                                                      │
     │ - Madelon (UCI 171): 500 features, but it is synthetic, with 480 noise  │
     │   probes. These aren't real tabular features.                           │
     │ - Bioresponse (OpenML 4134): 3,751 × 1,776, binary. Exceeds TabFM's     │
     │   500-feature limit.                                                    │
     │ - cnae-9 (OpenML 1468): only 1,080 × 856 rows. Too few for a 2,000-row  │
     │   background.                                                           │
     │                                                                         │
     │ HAR is the best fit because it has hundreds of real, dense, numeric     │
     │ features; it is a CC18 benchmark; it has a published subject-held-out   │
     │ split; it has six balanced classes; and it is small enough to train     │
     │ every model on the full training set.                                   │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Code changes (lime_study/)                                              │
     │                                                                         │
     │ New datasets.py: dataset loaders and specifications                     │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - DatasetSpec dataclass with these fields:                              │
     │   - name                                                                │
     │   - class_names                                                         │
     │   - feature_counts                                                      │
     │   - n_always_included (Covertype 10, HAR 0)                             │
     │   - categorical_start (Covertype 10, HAR None)                          │
     │   - scale_columns (which columns LR standardises)                       │
     │   - loader                                                              │
     │   - source metadata (URL, DOI)                                          │
     │ - load_covertype(): the existing protocol.load_controlled_covtype logic │
     │   moved here unchanged (10k sample, 80/20 split).                       │
     │ - load_har():                                                           │
     │   - Downloads https://archive.ics.uci.edu/static/public/240/human+activ │
     │     ity+recognition+using+smartphones.zip once to lime_study/data/      │
     │     (gitignored) and extracts the nested UCI HAR Dataset.zip.           │
     │   - Reads train/X_train.txt, y_train.txt, subject_train.txt and the     │
     │     matching test/ files with whitespace separation.                    │
     │   - Maps labels from 1–6 to 0–5 and takes class names from              │
     │     activity_labels.txt.                                                │
     │   - Takes column names from features.txt, de-duplicated: HAR repeats    │
     │     some bandsEnergy names, so a numeric suffix is added to keep        │
     │     DataFrame columns unique.                                           │
     │   - Asserts the shapes 7,352×561 and 2,947×561, and that the train and  │
     │     test subject sets don't overlap. Returns the subject arrays for     │
     │     metadata and records the zip's SHA-256.                             │
     │ - DATASETS = {"har": ..., "covertype": ...}, with DEFAULT_DATASET =     │
     │   "har".                                                                │
     │                                                                         │
     │ config.py                                                               │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - The Covertype-only dataset block (N_NUMERIC, CLASS_NAMES,             │
     │   FEATURE_COUNTS) moves into DatasetSpecs.                              │
     │ - Seeds, background size, sweep, LIME settings and N_JOBS stay.         │
     │ - Model factories take the spec: build_logistic_regression(spec)        │
     │   applies StandardScaler to spec.scale_columns. For HAR that is all     │
     │   columns (the features are already in [-1, 1], so scaling is harmless  │
     │   and consistent). max_iter=1000 stays. If HAR raises a                 │
     │   ConvergenceWarning, raise it for both datasets and document the       │
     │   change. XGB and RF are unchanged.                                     │
     │ - results_dir(dataset, sweep) returns results/<dataset>[/quick];        │
     │   figures_dir works the same way.                                       │
     │ - snapshot() includes the dataset spec.                                 │
     │                                                                         │
     │ protocol.py                                                             │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - feature_subset(spec, feature_count, n_features): always include the   │
     │   first n_always_included features, then a seeded permutation of the    │
     │   rest, so the subsets stay nested. For HAR this gives a seeded random  │
     │   nested 100 ⊂ 300 ⊂ 500.                                               │
     │ - Categorical positions are found with original_index >=                │
     │   spec.categorical_start; HAR has none, so every feature is             │
     │   quartile-discretised.                                                 │
     │ - Class names, cases per class and validation counts come from the      │
     │   spec. For HAR that is 18 cases, 1,350 explanations / 2,700 stability  │
     │   pairs / 15 summary rows per model.                                    │
     │ - run_model_experiment(model_name, dataset, quick). run_meta also       │
     │   records the dataset name, source, DOI, zip hash and train/test        │
     │   subject IDs.                                                          │
     │ - cli() gains --dataset.                                                │
     │                                                                         │
     │ run_all.py / plot_results.py                                            │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - Both take --dataset (default har) and pass it to the model            │
     │   subprocesses.                                                         │
     │ - Plots read feature counts from the spec. The figures are the same,    │

     ╭─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────╮
     │ Plan: Replace Covertype with UCI HAR (561 features) and document the    │
     │ dataset justification                                                   │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Context                                                                 │
     │                                                                         │
     │ The team decided the study needs a dataset with hundreds of features.   │
     │ Covertype has only 54. The replacement is UCI Human Activity            │
     │ Recognition Using Smartphones (HAR). The task is to justify that choice │
     │ with verified sources and switch lime_study/ over to it. Covertype is   │
     │ removed completely: no dataset option and no Covertype code. The        │
     │ earlier Covertype results stay in git history (commit 58c9cb4) and are  │
     │ overwritten by the HAR run.                                             │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Agreed decisions                                                        │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - Source/split: the original UCI download, using its official           │
     │   subject-based split. That is 7,352 training rows from 21 volunteers   │
     │   and 2,947 test rows from 9 volunteers, with no volunteer in both      │
     │   sets. OpenML 1478 is not used because it has no subject column.       │
     │ - HAR only. There is no --dataset flag or registry. HAR-specific values │
     │   sit in one "dataset block" in config.py, just as Covertype's did.     │
     │ - Perturbed-feature sweep: 100 / 300 / 500 nested subsets. This stays   │
     │   within TabFM's max_num_features=500 default, confirmed in             │
     │   tabfm/README.md and tabfm/tabfm/src/classifier_and_regressor.py:1105. │
     │   Models always predict on all 561 features.                            │

     │   background.                                                           │

     │                                                                         │

     │ - Replace the Covertype dataset block with HAR values:                  │
     │   - DATASET_NAME = "uci_har"                                            │
     │   - DATA_URL (the UCI zip) and DATA_DOI = "10.24432/C54S4K"             │

     │     "SITTING", "STANDING", "LAYING"). These are checked against         │
     │     activity_labels.txt at load time.                                   │
     │   - FEATURE_COUNTS = (100, 300, 500)                                    │
     │ - Remove N_NUMERIC, SAMPLE_SIZE and TEST_SIZE. HAR uses the official    │
     │   split, and every feature is continuous.                               │
     │ - Keep seeds, BACKGROUND_SIZE = 2_000, the sweep, the LIME settings and │
     │   N_JOBS.                                                               │
     │ - build_logistic_regression() becomes Pipeline([StandardScaler(),       │
     │   LogisticRegression(max_iter=1000, solver="lbfgs", random_state=42)]), │
     │   scaling every column with no ColumnTransformer. If HAR raises a       │
     │   ConvergenceWarning, increase max_iter and document the change. XGB    │
     │   and RF are unchanged.                                                 │
     │                                                                         │
     │ protocol.py                                                             │

     │   - Maps labels from 1–6 to 0–5.                                        │
     │   - Takes column names from features.txt, de-duplicated: HAR repeats    │
     │     some bandsEnergy names, so a numeric suffix is added.               │
     │   - Asserts the shapes 7,352×561 and 2,947×561, the class names, and    │
     │     that the train and test subjects don't overlap.                     │
     │   - Returns X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test, plus subject and hash     │
     │     metadata for run_meta.                                              │
     │ - feature_subset() becomes a seeded nested permutation of all 561       │
     │   features (100 ⊂ 300 ⊂ 500). The numeric/binary logic goes away.       │

❯ why is it doing a 70/30 split for training and testing and not 80/20?

⏺ The 70/30 split is HAR's official person-based partition (21 vs 9 volunteers),
  kept to prevent leakage and match prior published results—unlike our
  arbitrary 80/20 for Covertype. If 80/20 is needed throughout, I can instead
  create a seeded volunteer-level split (24 vs 6) to approximate that ratio
  without leakage.

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Which train/test split should HAR use? → Official 70/30 (Recommended)

⏺ User approved Claude's plan
  ⎿  Plan saved to: ~/.claude/plans/i-need-help-planning-merry-island.md · /plan
     to edit
     Plan: Replace Covertype with UCI HAR (561 features) and document the
     dataset justification

     Context

     The team decided the study needs a dataset with hundreds of features.
     Covertype has only 54. The replacement is UCI Human Activity Recognition
     Using Smartphones (HAR). The task is to justify that choice with verified
     sources and switch lime_study/ over to it. Covertype is removed completely:
     no dataset option and no Covertype code. The earlier Covertype results
     stay in git history (commit 58c9cb4) and are overwritten by the HAR run.

     Agreed decisions

     - Source/split: the original UCI download, using its official subject-based
       70/30 split. This was confirmed over a custom 80/20 for leakage-free,
       literature-comparable results. That is 7,352 training rows from 21
       volunteers and 2,947 test rows from 9 volunteers, with no volunteer in
       both sets. OpenML 1478 is not used because it has no subject column.
     - HAR only. There is no --dataset flag or registry. HAR-specific values sit
       in one "dataset block" in config.py, just as Covertype's did.
     - Perturbed-feature sweep: 100 / 300 / 500 nested subsets. This stays
       within TabFM's max_num_features=500 default, confirmed in tabfm/README.md
       and tabfm/tabfm/src/classifier_and_regressor.py:1105. Models always
       predict on all 561 features.
     - Runtime reporting: unchanged (mean ± std).

     Verified research (goes into lime_study/notes/dataset_justification.md)

     Each source was checked live during planning. The status column shows what
     was checked.

     #: 1
     Source: UCI dataset page: https://archive.ics.uci.edu/dataset/240 · DOI
     10.24432/C54S4K · CC BY 4.0
     Verified facts: 10,299 instances, 561-feature vector, no missing values, 6
     activities. Creators: Reyes-Ortiz, Anguita, Ghio, Oneto, Parra. Split: 70%
     of
      the 30 volunteers for training, 30% for testing

     437–442, ISBN 978-2-87419-081-0. PDF:
     https://www.esann.org/sites/default/files/proceedings/legacy/es2013-84.pdf
     Verified facts: 30 volunteers aged 19–48. Waist-mounted Samsung Galaxy S
     II, 50
     Hz accelerometer and gyroscope, 2.56 s windows with 50% overlap. 17
     signals;
     561 features. 70/30 split, 2,947 test patterns. Multiclass SVM reaches 96%
     Status: Full PDF read

     10.1080/01605682.2020.1865846 (arXiv:2001.11757)
     Verified facts: Repeated LIME runs vary. Supports the stability metric
     Status: arXiv verified; check volume/pages against the DOI
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     #: 8
     Source: Secondary sources for the subject split: arXiv:1811.00170,
     arXiv:2006.00367
     Verified facts: 21 training volunteers / 7,352 rows; 9 test volunteers /
     2,947
     rows
     Status: Search-verified; the loader also asserts these numbers

     Alternatives considered (verified) and why we rejected them:
     - Isolet (UCI 54): 7,797 × 617, 26 classes. Too many classes for 3 cases
       per class.
     - Madelon (UCI 171): synthetic, with 480 noise probes. These aren't real
       tabular features.
     - Bioresponse (OpenML 4134): 3,751 × 1,776. Exceeds TabFM's 500-feature
       limit.
     - cnae-9 (OpenML 1468): only 1,080 rows. Too few for a 2,000-row
       background.

     Code changes (lime_study/)

     config.py

     - Replace the Covertype dataset block with HAR values:
       - DATASET_NAME = "uci_har"
       - DATA_URL (the UCI zip) and DATA_DOI = "10.24432/C54S4K"
       - DATA_DIR = PACKAGE_DIR / "data"
       - CLASS_NAMES = ("WALKING", "WALKING_UPSTAIRS", "WALKING_DOWNSTAIRS",
         "SITTING", "STANDING", "LAYING"). These are checked against
         activity_labels.txt at load time.
       - FEATURE_COUNTS = (100, 300, 500)
     - Remove N_NUMERIC, SAMPLE_SIZE and TEST_SIZE. HAR uses the official split,
       and every feature is continuous.
     - Keep seeds, BACKGROUND_SIZE = 2_000, the sweep, the LIME settings and
       N_JOBS.
     - build_logistic_regression() becomes Pipeline([StandardScaler(),
       LogisticRegression(max_iter=1000, solver="lbfgs", random_state=42)]),
       scaling every column with no ColumnTransformer. If HAR raises a
       ConvergenceWarning, increase max_iter and document the change. XGB and RF
       are unchanged.

     protocol.py

     - Replace load_controlled_covtype() with load_har():
       - Downloads https://archive.ics.uci.edu/static/public/240/human+activity+
         recognition+using+smartphones.zip once to lime_study/data/ and extracts
         the nested UCI HAR Dataset.zip.
       - Reads train/X_train.txt, y_train.txt, subject_train.txt and the
         matching test/ files with whitespace separation.
       - Maps labels from 1–6 to 0–5.
       - Takes column names from features.txt, de-duplicated: HAR repeats some
         bandsEnergy names, so a numeric suffix is added.
       - Asserts the shapes 7,352×561 and 2,947×561, the class names, and that
         the train and test subjects don't overlap.
       - Returns X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test, plus subject and hash
         metadata for run_meta.
     - feature_subset() becomes a seeded nested permutation of all 561 features
       (100 ⊂ 300 ⊂ 500). The numeric/binary logic goes away.
     - No categorical features: remove categorical_features and
       categorical_names from the explainer call, so every feature is
       quartile-discretised.
     - Counts per model become 18 cases (3 × 6 classes): 1,350 explanations,
       2,700 stability pairs, 15 summary rows. validate() already derives these
       from CLASS_NAMES and the sweep.
     - run_meta also records the source URL, DOI, zip SHA-256 and the train/test
       subject IDs.

     run_all.py, plot_results.py, models/

     No structural change. The plots already read FEATURE_COUNTS from config.
     Fix any Covertype wording in docstrings and labels.

     Housekeeping

     - Delete the old Covertype outputs in lime_study/results/ and
       lime_study/figures/ (they remain in git history) before the HAR run, so
       stale files can't mix with the new ones.
     - Add lime_study/data/ to .gitignore.
     - Update lime_study/README.md and
       lime_study/notes/lime_study_rebuild_plan.md for HAR: the dataset, split,
       feature counts and run counts. Add a line saying Covertype was dropped in
       favour of a high-dimensional dataset, with a link to the justification
       note. Leave experiment_handoff.md and methodology_comparison.md as
       historical records, adding a one-line "superseded" note to each.
     - Write lime_study/notes/dataset_justification.md for the team
       (Amelia/Hugh). It contains the justification paragraph, the
       verified-sources table with its status column, the rejected alternatives,
       and a "how to verify" checklist with the DOI, PDF, OpenML links and the
       author affiliations stated in the paper (University of Genova DITEN; UPC
       CETpD).

     Team documentation (outside lime_study/)

     - data_sources/potential_data_sources: append a HAR entry in the file's
       existing plain-text format: name line, then indented Link:, Info: and AI
       Ops: lines, separated from the previous entry by a blank line. Proposed
       content:
     Human Activity Recognition Using Smartphones (UCI HAR)
         Link: https://archive.ics.uci.edu/dataset/240/human+activity+recognitio
     n+using+smartphones
         Info: 10,299 rows with 561 numeric features (time- and frequency-domain
     statistics from smartphone accelerometer/gyroscope signals), 6 activity
     classes, no missing values, recorded from 30 volunteers with an official
     subject-based train/test split (7,352 / 2,947). Also on OpenML (ID 1478)
     and part of the OpenML-CC18 benchmark suite. DOI 10.24432/C54S4K
         AI Ops: CHOSEN DATASET (replaces Covertype). Hundreds of real, dense
     features for the dimensionality sweep; 100/300/500-feature LIME subsets
     stay within TabFM's 500-feature limit
       Also add "(previous dataset, replaced by UCI HAR)" to Covertype's AI Ops
       line.
     - log_entries/20-10-2026.md: a new log entry in the markdown style of
       18-10-2026.md (# title, ## sections, bullet points). Sections:
       a. Why we changed dataset: Covertype has only 54 features (10 numeric +
          44 binary indicators), so "high-dimensional" in the paper title wasn't
          credible, and the 17 Oct meeting set the goal of hundreds of
          features.
       b. What we changed to: UCI HAR. 561 features, 10,299 rows, 6 classes, 30
          volunteers, DOI and ESANN 2013 citation.
       c. Why HAR: real dense numeric features rather than synthetic ones like
          Madelon; an official subject-held-out split, which avoids leakage;
          balanced classes (13.7–18.9%); a CC18 benchmark; small enough to train
          every model on the full training set; rejected alternatives in brief.
       d. Why 100 / 300 / 500 perturbed features:
          - The models always use all 561 features; only LIME's perturbed subset
            changes, so model quality is held fixed.
          - The steps span roughly 1/5, 1/2 and nearly all of the feature space,
            giving the same "low / mid / full" structure as the old 18/36/54
            sweep.
          - The subsets are nested (100 ⊂ 300 ⊂ 500), so each step only adds
            features.
          - 500 is TabFM's documented max_num_features, so TabFM can be added
            later under identical conditions.
          - The 100-feature step is already above the "hundreds" threshold from
            the 17 Oct to-do.
       e. Why the split is 70/30 rather than our old 80/20:
          - It is HAR's official split, made by volunteer: 21 training
            volunteers (7,352 rows) and 9 test volunteers (2,947 rows).
          - Keeping each person on one side prevents leakage, because windows
            from the same person are highly correlated.
          - Using the published split keeps our accuracies comparable with the
            literature.
          - The 80/20 for Covertype was our own choice, made only because
            Covertype had no official split.
       f. What else changed:
          - Covertype code removed, with its results kept in git history.
          - Explanations per model change from 1,575 to 1,350 (18 cases).
          - No categorical features any more.
          - Link to lime_study/notes/dataset_justification.md for the sources.

     Verification

     1. python -m lime_study.run_all --quick downloads HAR, passes the loader
        assertions (shapes, class names, disjoint subjects), and writes
        results/quick and figures/quick.
     2. Run grep -ri covtype\|covertype lime_study --include=*.py. It should
        return nothing.
     3. Run the full study in the background. Expected time is roughly 10–25 min
        per model.
     4. Check that:
        - each model has 1,350 raw rows and 15 summary rows, with 45 combined
          rows;
        - there are no NaNs;
        - test accuracy is plausible (around 0.9 or higher; the paper's SVM
          reached 96%);
        - runtime increases with both budget and feature count;
        - re-running the quick study gives identical fidelity and stability.
     5. Look at the regenerated figures.

❯ just realised that your reasoning behind the 100/300/500 split for features
  was becuase we used 18/36/54 before. this isn't a valid answer

⏺ You're right: "we did it before" isn't a justification. Here is reasoning that
  stands on its own:

  1. The upper bound of 500 has a concrete reason. It is TabFM's documented
     max_num_features limit. Going above 500 would mean TabFM sees a
     feature-subsampled input while the other models don't, which breaks the
     controlled comparison. 500 also covers 89% of HAR's 561 features.
  2. The lower bound of 100 sets the "high-dimensional" floor. The study's
     premise is features in the hundreds. Starting at 100 means even the
     smallest condition meets that requirement, and LIME's 10-feature
     explanation is then a small slice (10%) of what was perturbed.
  3. 300 makes the steps evenly spaced. Equal 200-feature steps give three
     equally spaced points. Three is the minimum needed to tell a linear trend
     from a curving one (for example, stability that drops sharply and then
     levels off).
  4. Crossed with the budgets, the grid spans meaningful sample-to-feature
     ratios. LIME fits a ridge surrogate to num_samples perturbations over d
     features. With budgets of 500–10,000 and d of 100–500, the ratio runs from
     1:1 (500 samples, 500 features, where the surrogate is barely determined)
     to 100:1 (10,000 samples, 100 features). LIME's default kernel width also
     grows with dimension (0.75·√d = 7.5, 13.0 and 16.8). Garreau & von Luxburg
     (2020) show this kind of parameter choice can make LIME miss important
     features, so these are the regimes where fidelity and stability are
     expected to change.

  One honest caveat: no source prescribes exactly 100/300/500. The two ends are
  justified (TabFM's limit and the "hundreds" floor), but the middle value is a
  design choice for even spacing. The paper should present it as a design
  decision, not as something taken from the literature.

  I'll replace the old reasoning in the log entry and the justification note:

⏺ I replaced the "same as the old 18/36/54 sweep" reasoning in both
  log_entries/20-10-2026.md and lime_study/notes/dataset_justification.md. Here
  is the new justification:

  - 500 (top): it's TabFM's documented feature limit. Going above it would mean
    TabFM sees fewer features than the other models, so the comparison would no
    longer be fair. 500 still covers 89% of HAR's 561 features.
  - 100 (bottom): it keeps even the smallest condition in the "hundreds of
    features" range the study is about.
  - 300 (middle): it gives equal 200-feature steps. Three evenly spaced points
    are the minimum needed to tell a straight-line trend from a curving one.
  - Why these ranges are interesting: combined with the 500–10,000 sample
    budgets, the grid runs from 1 perturbation sample per feature (barely enough
    for LIME's surrogate) up to 100 per feature. LIME's default kernel width
    also grows with the feature count (7.5 → 13.0 → 16.8). Garreau & von Luxburg
    (2020) show settings like these affect which features LIME finds, so this
    is where we'd expect fidelity and stability to change.

  No paper prescribes exactly 100/300/500. The two ends are tied to real
  constraints, but 300 is our own choice for even spacing. The paper should
  present it as a design decision rather than claim it comes from the
  literature.

  If the team wants the middle step on firmer ground, we could add more points
  (for example 100/200/300/400/500). That would show the trend's shape more
  clearly, at about 5/3 of the run time.

  The full HAR run is still going in the background. I'll report back when it
  finishes.

⏺ The full HAR run finished in about 23 minutes, and every check passed:
  - 1,350 explanations and 2,700 stability pairs per model, 45 summary rows, and
    no NaNs.
  - Re-running a model gives identical fidelity, weights and stability.

  Test accuracy: Logistic Regression 0.955, XGBoost 0.941, Random Forest 0.928.
  That's plausible next to the original paper's 96% SVM, and Logistic Regression
  is now the strongest model rather than the weakest.

  The figures are in lime_study/figures/.

  What the results show:
  - Runtime grows with both the sample budget and the feature count: about 0.02s
    at 100 features with 500 samples, up to about 0.9s at 500 features with
    10,000 samples. At 500 features the three models take almost the same time,
    so LIME's own work outweighs the cost of the models' predictions.
  - Fidelity is low for all three models (R² roughly 0.1–0.3) and barely changes
    with budget. XGBoost has the highest fidelity and Logistic Regression the
    lowest.
  - Stability rises steadily with budget and falls as the feature count goes up.
    With 500 features and only 500 samples it collapses (top-5 Jaccard
    0.01–0.03), meaning the five repeated runs pick almost entirely different
    top features. This is the 1-sample-per-feature case from the justification,
    so the data supports that reasoning.

  Two things to know before writing the results section:
  - Spearman goes negative in that collapsed case (Random Forest: −0.56 at 500
    features / 500 samples). Spearman is computed over all features either run
    selected. When two runs pick nearly separate sets, each run's weights are
    zero exactly where the other's are non-zero, which forces a negative score.
    A negative value there means "no agreement", not "opposite rankings". Use
    Jaccard as the main stability measure and treat Spearman as secondary.
  - The Covertype results files were deleted from lime_study/ and replaced by
    HAR results, so git status shows them as modified or deleted. They're still
    in commit 58c9cb4.

  Files changed:
  - Code: config.py (lime_study/config.py) and protocol.py
    (lime_study/protocol.py) now hold the HAR loader, which downloads the data,
    de-duplicates the 42 repeated feature names, and checks the shapes and that
    no volunteer is in both splits. requirements-pinned.txt
    (lime_study/requirements-pinned.txt) adds certifi so the download works on
    python.org's macOS Python.
  - New docs: dataset_justification.md
    (lime_study/notes/dataset_justification.md) and the log entry 20-10-2026.md
    (log_entries/20-10-2026.md).
  - Updated docs: the HAR entry in potential_data_sources
    (data_sources/potential_data_sources), the README (lime_study/README.md),
    the rebuild plan (lime_study/notes/lime_study_rebuild_plan.md), and
    "superseded" notes on the two old Covertype notes.
  - .gitignore now ignores lime_study/data/, where the 60 MB download is cached.

❯ the data is split into 70/30 because the dataset already does it, i think we

  70/30 Split
  Another widely used ratio allocates 70% of the data for training and 30% for
  testing.

  When to use it: If you want a slightly more confident estimate of your
  model's generalization performance, increasing the test set size to 30% can
  help. This might be preferred if your dataset isn't massive, and you want to
  ensure the test set is adequately representative. It's also a good choice if
  the model you are training is relatively simple and doesn't require enormous
  amounts of data to learn effectively.
  can you update the notes/logs, or where we explained why 70/30 with one or
  two lines on this

     to edit
     Plan: Replace log-scaled axes with linear axes in the figures

     Context

     The user finds the figures misleading. lime_study/plot_results.py puts
     three axes on a log scale:
     - the sample-budget x axis (_format_budget_axis, line ~116), used in the
       grid and the Spearman figure;
     - the runtime y axis (line ~149);
     - the runtime x axis in cost_vs_quality (line ~177).

     On a log scale, budgets of 500 / 1k / 2.5k / 5k / 10k look evenly spaced
     even though the gaps differ tenfold. Runtimes from 0.02 s to 0.9 s are
     squashed the same way. Readers see linear runtime growth as curved, and
     small-budget changes look as large as big ones. Goal: every axis plots on a
     true linear scale.

     Changes (only lime_study/plot_results.py)

     1. _format_budget_axis:
        - Remove set_xscale("log") and minorticks_off().
        - Use linear x from 0 to 10,500, with ticks at 0, 2.5k, 5k, 7.5k and
          10k, labelled with the existing k formatter.
        - The data markers still sit at the true budgets (500, 1k, 2.5k, 5k,
          10k). The 500 and 1k ticks are not labelled separately, because they
          would overlap.
     2. Runtime row of the grid:
        - Remove set_yscale("log"), _plain_log_ticks and the
          set_ylim(bottom=min/2) workaround.
        - Set the linear y axis to bottom=0. Keep the y axis shared across the
          row, so 100, 300 and 500 features are directly comparable.
        - The ±std bands no longer need clipping.
     3. plot_cost_vs_quality:
        - Remove set_xscale("log") and _plain_log_ticks.
        - Use linear runtime on the x axis from 0, keeping the axis shared
          across the three panels.
     4. Clean-up: delete _plain_log_ticks and the now-unused
        FuncFormatter/NullFormatter imports if nothing else uses them.

     No re-run is needed. Redraw from the saved results with python -m
     lime_study.run_all --plots-only.

     Update one line in lime_study/notes/lime_study_rebuild_plan.md and

⏺ Here's what I found on both concerns.

  1. Spearman going negative is an artefact of how it's computed

  Each LIME explanation selects 10 features. We compute Spearman over the union
  of the two runs' selected features, treating a feature the other run didn't
  select as weight 0. When the two runs select mostly different features, each
  vector is non-zero exactly where the other is zero, and that forces a negative
  correlation. I recomputed all 8,100 repeat pairs from the saved weights:

  ┌────────────────────────────────┬─────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │  Features shared by the two    │    Mean     │ Share of pairs that are │
  │        runs (out of 10)        │  Spearman   │         negative        │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ 0                              │ −0.21       │ 58%                     │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ 1                              │ −0.03       │ 42%                     │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ 3                              │ 0.29        │ 11%                     │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ 5                              │ 0.55        │ 3%                      │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ 10                             │ 0.92        │ 0%                      │
  └────────────────────────────────┴─────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  - Spearman correlates at 0.85 with the number of shared features. In this
    setup it is mostly measuring overlap, which Jaccard already does.
  - The worst case, Random Forest with 500 features and 500 samples (−0.56), is
    where the runs share a median of 0 features.
  - The runs never actually disagree: when they share a feature, its weight has
    the same sign 98.7% of the time. So a negative value means "no common
    features", not "opposite explanations". As it stands the metric is
    misleading.

  There are standard measures built for comparing top-k lists that partly
  disagree, and I've verified them:
  - Rank-biased overlap (RBO): Webber, Moffat & Zobel, ACM TOIS 28(4), 2010, DOI
    10.1145/1852102.1852106
    (https://people.eng.unimelb.edu.au/ammoffat/abstracts/wmz10acmtois.html). It
    handles features that appear in only one list, gives more weight to the top
    ranks, and ranges from 0 (nothing in common) to 1 (identical ranking). It
    can never go negative.
  - Top-k distance measures: Fagin, Kumar & Sivakumar, "Comparing top k lists",
    SIAM J. Discrete Math. 17(1):134–160, 2003 (PDF
    (https://www.biz.uiowa.edu/faculty/nstreet/Fagin03.pdf)). These are the
    formal basis for the same problem.

  No LIME re-run is needed for any fix, because every explanation's weights are
  saved in *_raw.csv.

  2. Better support for the 70/30 split (peer-reviewed, verified)

  - Dobbin & Simon (2011), "Optimally splitting cases for training and testing
    high dimensional classifiers", BMC Medical Genomics 4:31, DOI
    10.1186/1755-8794-4-31 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3090739/).
    For high-dimensional classifiers they found that giving about 2/3 of the
    data to training "was close to optimal for reasonable sized datasets (n
    ≥ 100) with strong signals (85% or greater accuracy)". HAR fits both
    conditions (n = 10,299; accuracy 93–95%), and 70/30 is close to 2/3.
  - Saeb et al. (2017), "The need to approximate the use-case in clinical
    machine learning", GigaScience 6(5):1–9, DOI 10.1093/gigascience/gix019
    (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5441397/). Splitting by record
    instead of by subject "often massively overestimates the prediction
    accuracy", and almost half of the studies they reviewed made that mistake.
    This supports using HAR's split by volunteer.
  - Gholamiangonabadi, Kiselov & Grolinger (2020), IEEE Access 8:133982–133994
    (DBLP (https://dblp.org/pid/271/6834)). This is HAR-specific work arguing
    for subject-held-out evaluation. I confirmed the citation but couldn't read
    the full text.
  - A weaker option: Joseph (2022), "Optimal ratio for data splitting" (DeepAI
    (https://deepai.org/publication/optimal-ratio-for-data-splitting)), proposes
    a √p:1 rule. Sources disagree on the exact formula, so I wouldn't cite it.

  These can replace the apxml link as the paper's citations. Before I change
  anything, two decisions:

     reason that can be explained why it would make sense it would be in the
     negatives
     · Where should the new 70/30 sources go? → Replace apxml (Recommended)

  agree only by chance. That shows what a negative value actually means.

      26 -**Why the split is 70/30 rather than our old 80/20.** It is HAR

⏺ I've replaced the apxml link with the peer-reviewed sources in both
  log_entries/20-10-2026.md and dataset_justification.md
  (lime_study/notes/dataset_justification.md):
  - Dobbin & Simon (2011) for the ratio: about 2/3 for training is close to
    optimal for high-dimensional classifiers when n ≥ 100 and accuracy is 85% or
    higher, which HAR meets.
  - Saeb et al. (2017) and Gholamiangonabadi et al. (2020) for splitting by
    subject.

  All three are added to the verified-sources table. I read the abstracts of the
  first two; I could confirm only the citation of the third.

  Is there a valid reason for negative Spearman? Yes

  A negative value here means the two runs agreed no more than chance would. It
  does not mean they disagreed. The reason is how we handle features that only
  one run selected: we give them a weight of 0. For this metric, "no agreement"
  therefore sits below zero, not at zero.

  To test this, I simulated pairs of explanations that pick their 10 features
  completely at random, using each model's own weights. This is what the metric
  scores for pure chance:

  ┌────────────────┬───────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │     Model      │ Chance level (100 / 300 / │ Observed at 500 samples   │
  │                │       500 features)       │     (100 / 300 / 500)     │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ XGBoost        │ −0.08 / −0.14 / −0.24     │ +0.46 / +0.35 / −0.07     │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Logistic       │ 0.00 / −0.05 / −0.08      │ +0.32 / +0.13 / −0.02     │
  │ Regression     │                           │                           │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Random Forest  │ −0.49 / −0.57 / −0.71     │ +0.42 / −0.05 / −0.55     │
  └────────────────┴───────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  - Random Forest's −0.55 is actually slightly better than chance (−0.71). At
    500 features and 500 samples, LIME's top 10 is close to random for every
    model. That is a real finding, and it matches Jaccard ≈ 0.01–0.03.
  - The chance level is different for each model, from about 0 to −0.71. It
    depends on whether a model's LIME weights are mostly the same sign (Random
    Forest) or mixed (Logistic Regression), because that decides where the zeros
    rank. As a result, raw Spearman values can't be compared fairly across
    models: the same score means different things for Random Forest and Logistic
    Regression.

  So the negative values can be explained in the paper. Something like:
  "negative values arise because unselected features are zero-filled; at
  chance-level overlap the expected value is below zero (−0.08 to −0.71
  depending on the model), so they indicate near-random agreement rather than
  opposing explanations."

  That caveat is the reason I'd still change the metric. You have two options:
  1. Keep Spearman, but show each model's chance level on the plot as a dashed
     line, and explain it in the text. This keeps the current metric, but
     readers have to adjust for a different baseline per model.
  2. Replace it with rank-biased overlap (RBO) (Webber et al. 2010). Chance
     level is about 0 for every model and it can't go negative, so models
     compare directly. It still measures rank agreement, unlike Jaccard.

  Both can be computed from the saved results without re-running LIME. Which do
  you want?

  how does RBO change this?

  model, what happens if we use absolute weights, and what RBO gives on our
  data.

⏺ Short version: the signed weights are the problem, taking absolute values
  fixes the comparison between models but not the meaning of the scale, and RBO
  fixes both.

  Why the same Spearman score means different things for different models

  LIME weights can be positive (pushes toward the predicted class) or negative
  (pushes away). The share of positive weights differs a lot by model:

  ┌─────────────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │        Model        │ Positive weights │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Random Forest       │ 93%              │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ XGBoost             │ 71%              │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Logistic Regression │ 60%              │
  └─────────────────────┴──────────────────┘

  Our Spearman gives a weight of 0 to any feature the other run didn't select.
  Where that 0 lands in the ranking depends on the signs:

  - Random Forest: almost every weight is positive, so 0 is the lowest value. A
    feature ranked near the top in run A but missing from run B is ranked last
    in B. If the runs pick different features, they look like opposite rankings,
    so the score is strongly negative.
  - Logistic Regression: the weights are mixed, so 0 sits in the middle of the
    ranking. Missing features land mid-table and push the score toward 0.

  So two runs that agree only at random score −0.71 for Random Forest but −0.08
  for Logistic Regression. This changes the conclusions. At 500 features and
  1,000 samples:

  ┌─────────────────────┬─────────────────┬─────────┬──────┐
  │                     │ Signed Spearman │ Jaccard │ RBO  │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────┼──────┤
  │ Random Forest       │ −0.10           │ 0.25    │ 0.32 │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────┼──────┤
  │ Logistic Regression │ +0.21           │ 0.21    │ 0.29 │
  └─────────────────────┴─────────────────┴─────────┴──────┘

  Spearman says Logistic Regression is far more stable. Jaccard and RBO both say
  Random Forest is slightly more stable. The Spearman gap comes from the sign
  mix, not from stability.

  Can we standardise the signs? Partly

  If we rank by absolute weight (|w|), the chance level becomes the same for
  every model (−0.84 at 500 features), so models can be compared fairly. Two
  problems remain:
  - The scale is still hard to read. Random agreement scores −0.84 rather than
    0, because missing features always rank last, and that baseline also changes
    with the feature count. A reader sees −0.4 and thinks "disagreement" when
    it is actually above chance.
  - It throws away direction. We'd report direction separately, and that number
    is good: when two runs share a feature, its sign matches 98.7% of the time.

  How RBO changes this

  Rank-biased overlap doesn't fill in zeros. It takes each run's top-10 list
  (ordered by |weight|) and asks at each depth 1, 2, …, 10 what fraction of the
  top d features the two lists share. It then averages those fractions, giving
  the top positions the most weight. A feature that's missing from one list just
  adds no overlap. It never counts as "ranked last".

  - No sign issue: it ranks by |weight|, so signs don't matter.
  - Same baseline for every model: random agreement scores about 0.01 for all
    three, identical lists score 1, and it can't go negative.
  - Adds something Jaccard doesn't: Jaccard asks "are the same 5 features in the
    top 5?". RBO also rewards keeping the same order, and it uses all 10
    features.

  On our data RBO moves sensibly from 500 to 10,000 samples (e.g. XGBoost at 500
  features: 0.09 → 0.57 → 0.74 → 0.81 → 0.84) and agrees with Jaccard about
  which model is more stable.

  One caveat: I used the version that stops at our 10 features and is rescaled
  to 0–1, with top-weighting p = 0.9. The original paper (Webber et al. 2010)
  defines an extrapolated version for open-ended lists, so the methodology
  should state the variant and p.

  My recommendation: replace Spearman with RBO, keep Jaccard as the main
  stability measure, and report sign agreement on shared features as a third
  number for whether explanations keep the same direction. All of it can be
  computed from the saved results with no LIME re-run, and I'd update the
  stability figure and docs to match. Do you want me to go ahead?

❯ are we using spearman or jaccard at the moment?

⏺ Both are computed right now, but Jaccard is the main one:

  - Top-5 Jaccard is the stability measure in the main figure. It's the bottom
    row of lime_scaling_grid.png (lime_study/figures/lime_scaling_grid.png) and
    the y-axis of cost_vs_quality.png (lime_study/figures/cost_vs_quality.png).
  - Spearman only appears in its own secondary figure, stability_spearman.png
    (lime_study/figures/stability_spearman.png), and in the spearman_mean /
    spearman_std columns of the summary CSVs.

  Both are calculated in pairwise_stability() in protocol.py
  (lime_study/protocol.py), for every pair of repeat runs, and saved to
  *_stability.csv.

  So the Spearman problem doesn't affect the main results or figures. It only
  matters for the secondary figure and for anything written about Spearman in
  the paper. If you swap it for RBO, only that figure and the Spearman columns
  change.
❯ so why is stability shown measured in spearman in the graphs?

⏺ Because the team's original method asked for it, and I carried it over:

  - The 7 Oct log (log_entries/07-10-2026.md) proposed Spearman rank correlation
    as "one useful measure" of stability across repeated runs, and checking
    whether the same top 5 features appear (Jaccard) as a second option.
  - The shared protocol (experiments/controlled_lime_protocol.py) implemented
    both, and the handoff notes describe stability as "pairwise Spearman
    correlation and top-5 Jaccard".
  - When I rebuilt everything in lime_study/, the plan you approved kept both.
    Jaccard went into the main figure, and I added Spearman as a separate figure
    (stability_spearman.png) so that the second measure from the methodology
    had results to report.

  So it's there to follow the planned methodology, not because Spearman is the
  better measure. Given what we've found (its scale depends on each model's
  weight signs, so negative values aren't comparable between models), there's
  little reason to keep it as is. You can:

  1. Replace it with RBO (my recommendation). You keep a rank-based second
     measure, but it reads correctly: 0 means chance-level agreement and 1 means
     identical explanations.
  2. Remove the Spearman figure and report Jaccard only, with one line in the
     methodology on why Spearman was dropped.
  3. Keep it with each model's chance level drawn as a dashed line and an
     explanation in the text.

  Whichever you pick, the methodology section (Amelia's) should be updated to
  match, since the original plan named Spearman.

❯ is RBO any better than Jaccard?

⏺ I'll check how much RBO actually adds over Jaccard on our data:

⏺ Bash(cd "/Users/ExtraAccount/Documents/college
      subjects/block_10/xAI/xAI_Project/XAI" && .venv/bin/python - <<'EOF'
      import json, itertools, numpy as np, pandas as pd…)
  ⎿  (No output)

⏺ On our data, it's slightly better as a measurement but leads to the same
  conclusions. Jaccard should stay the main measure.

  What RBO adds

  - It notices order. In 20.5% of repeat pairs the top-5 features are the same
    set, so Jaccard scores them a perfect 1.0. But in 60% of those pairs the
    five features are in a different order. Jaccard can't see that, and RBO
    scores it lower.
  - It uses all 10 features, not just the top 5, with more weight on the top
    positions.
  - It's finer-grained. Top-5 Jaccard can only take 6 values (0, 1/9, 1/4, 3/7,
    2/3, 1). RBO took 2,530 different values across our pairs.

  Where it adds almost nothing

  - The overall conclusions are the same. Averaged over each model, feature
    count and budget, RBO and Jaccard correlate at 0.986. They disagree on which
    model is most or least stable in only 1 of 15 conditions.
  - Deeper agreement is rare. When the top 5 share nothing (Jaccard = 0), RBO
    finds meaningful agreement further down the top 10 in only 0.6% of pairs.

  Where Jaccard is better

  - Simpler to explain: "the share of top-5 features two runs have in common"
    needs no further definition.
  - No tuning setting. RBO needs a top-weighting value (p = 0.9) and a choice of
    variant, which a reviewer could question.
  - Common in LIME stability work, so readers will recognise it.

  Recommendation

  - Keep Jaccard as the main stability measure. It is already what the main
    figures use.
  - If you want a second measure, use RBO instead of Spearman. Its one real
    contribution is checking whether the order of the top features is stable,
    not just which features they are. It also never gives misleading negative
    values.
  - If page space is tight, drop the second measure entirely and report Jaccard
    alone. Add one sentence saying a rank-based check (RBO) gave the same
    conclusions (r = 0.99), so the result doesn't depend on the choice of
    measure.

  Whichever you choose, Spearman should go, because its values can't be compared
  across models.

      45  - Logistic Regression now standardises all features (previously

      48 +                                                               
      49 +## Stability metric: Spearman removed, top-5 Jaccard only      
      50 +- **Decision:** stability is now measured with **top-5 Jaccard 
         +only**: the share of top-5 features two repeated LIME runs have
         + in common (intersection over union). Spearman rank correlation
         + has been removed from the code, the results files and the figu
         +res.                                                           
      51 +- **Why Spearman was there:** the 7 Oct plan suggested both Spe
         +arman and top-5 agreement, so the protocol computed both.      
      52 +- **What went wrong with it:** each explanation selects only 10
         + features. To compare two runs, Spearman gives a weight of 0 to
         + any feature the other run didn't select. Where that 0 falls in
         + the ranking depends on how many of a model's LIME weights are 
         +positive (Random Forest 93%, XGBoost 71%, Logistic Regression 6
         +0%).                                                           
      53 +  - When two runs share no features, the score is pushed negati
         +ve. At 0 shared features the mean Spearman was −0.21, and overa
         +ll it correlated 0.85 with simple overlap.                     
      54 +  - Pure chance-level agreement scores −0.71 for Random Forest 
         +but only −0.08 for Logistic Regression (simulated at 500 featur
         +es). The same value therefore means different things for differ
         +ent models, and it reversed the model comparison: at 500 featur
         +es / 1,000 samples, Spearman ranked Logistic Regression as more
         + stable (+0.21 vs −0.10), while Jaccard showed Random Forest sl
         +ightly ahead (0.25 vs 0.21).                                   
      55 +  - Negative values did **not** mean the explanations disagreed
         +. When two runs shared a feature, its sign matched 98.7% of the
         + time.                                                         
      56 +- **Why Jaccard alone is enough:**                             
      57 +  - It has no sign problem.                                    
      58 +  - 0 always means "no top features in common", for every model
         +.                                                              
      59 +  - It is simple to explain and commonly used for LIME stabilit
         +y.                                                             
      60 +  - A rank-aware alternative (rank-biased overlap, Webber et al
         +. 2010) gave the same conclusions (r = 0.99 with Jaccard across
         + all conditions), so a second measure wouldn't change the findi
         +ngs. This can be mentioned in one sentence in the paper.       
      61 +- **What changed in the results:** nothing was re-run. The Spea
         +rman column was dropped from the saved stability/summary files,
         + and the Jaccard values are unchanged. `stability_spearman.png`
         + has been removed.                                             
      62 +- **For the methodology section:** describe stability as top-5 
         +Jaccard only, and if needed note why Spearman was not used.    

⏺ Spearman is gone and stability is now measured with top-5 Jaccard only. The
  log entry explains the change and the reasons.

  Code
  - protocol.py (lime_study/protocol.py) only calculates Jaccard now
    (pairwise_jaccard), and the summary tables no longer have Spearman columns.
  - plot_results.py (lime_study/plot_results.py) no longer draws the Spearman
    figure.
  - A quick test run of the new code worked: the stability file now only has a
    top5_jaccard column.

  Results (nothing re-run)
  - I removed the Spearman column from the saved stability and summary files and
    rebuilt the summaries with the new code.
  - I checked that the Jaccard and runtime values are unchanged and that all the
    row-count checks still pass.
  - stability_spearman.png/.pdf is deleted. The other figures were redrawn, and
    their contents are the same, since they already used Jaccard.

  Docs
  - log_entries/20-10-2026.md has a new section, "Stability metric: Spearman
    removed, top-5 Jaccard only". It covers:
    - why Spearman was there originally (the 7 Oct plan);
    - why its scores depend on each model's weight signs, with the numbers;
    - the example where it reversed the model comparison;
    - why negative values didn't mean the explanations disagreed;
    - why Jaccard alone is enough, noting that RBO gave the same conclusions (r
      = 0.99).
  - The README (lime_study/README.md) and the rebuild plan
    (lime_study/notes/lime_study_rebuild_plan.md) now describe stability as
    Jaccard only. The plan's one remaining Spearman mention is a note saying it
    was dropped, with a pointer to the log.

  The methodology section (Amelia) should describe stability as top-5 Jaccard
  only. The log entry has the reasoning if a reviewer asks why Spearman isn't
  used.

  Top-5 Jaccard stability (1 = identical top 5 across repeated runs, 0 = no

  ┌──────────┬─────────┬─────────────────────┬───────────────┬─────────┐

  │ 300      │ 500     │ 0.11                │ 0.19          │ 0.32    │

  ├──────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────┤
