  ├──────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────┤
  │ 500      │ 500     │ 0.01                │ 0.01          │ 0.03    │
  ├──────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────┤
  │ 500      │ 2,500   │ 0.42                │ 0.48          │ 0.66    │
  ├──────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────┤
  │ 500      │ 10,000  │ 0.65                │ 0.67          │ 0.75    │
  └──────────┴─────────┴─────────────────────┴───────────────┴─────────┘

  What it shows
  - More samples help, but with diminishing returns. Most of the gain comes by
    about 2,500 samples, and going from 5,000 to 10,000 adds little but doubles
    the runtime.
  - More perturbed features means less stability. At 500 features with 500
    samples, LIME's top 5 is essentially random (Jaccard 0.01–0.03), because
    that budget is only 1 sample per feature.
  - The most stable model changes with dimensionality. Random Forest leads at
    100 features, but XGBoost leads at 300 and 500. Logistic Regression is the
    least stable throughout, even though it's the most accurate model.
  - Runtime grows in a straight line with samples and gets steeper with more
    features, from about 0.02s at 100 features with 500 samples to about 0.9s at
    500 features with 10,000 samples. At 500 features all three models take
    almost the same time, so LIME's own work dominates.

  One thing on the figure: in a few panels the shaded ±1 std band for Jaccard
  goes above 1.0, although Jaccard can't actually exceed 1. That happens because
  the spread across cases is large. I can clip the bands to the valid 0–1
  range, or switch them to the interquartile range, if you want that tidied
  before it goes in the paper.

⏺ Here are the steps for the current version of the code. It has changed since I
  last described it: someone added an interleaved run design, held-out
  fidelity, 50,000-sample reference explanations, sensitivity variants and a
  strict version check. One consequence is that the full results currently saved
  in lime_study/results/ are in the old format, so they need a fresh full run.

  1. One-time setup

  cd "/Users/ExtraAccount/Documents/college
  subjects/block_10/xAI/xAI_Project/XAI"
  source .venv/bin/activate
  pip install -r lime_study/requirements-pinned.txt   # your venv already
  matches (checked: 0 mismatches)
  rm -f ~/.matplotlib/fontlist-*.json                 # fixes the stale font
  cache that breaks plotting

  Full runs refuse to start if your package or Python versions differ from
  requirements-pinned.txt (Python 3.13). Yours currently match.

  2. Smoke test (about 7 minutes)

  python -m lime_study.run_all --quick

  This writes to lime_study/results/quick/ and lime_study/figures/quick/. The
  first run also downloads the dataset (~60 MB). It isn't for the paper; it just
  confirms everything works.

  3. Full study (for the paper)

  caffeinate -i python -m lime_study.run_all

  - caffeinate -i stops the Mac from sleeping mid-run.
  - Expect this to take much longer than before, roughly 1.5–2 hours. That's my
    estimate, not measured. The sweep is now 4 feature counts × 30 cases (3,000
    timed explanations per model), plus two 50,000-sample reference explanations
    per case and feature count.
  - Keep the machine idle: close browsers and other heavy apps, and keep it
    plugged in. Runtime is one of the measurements. The run records speed
    probes, so you can check for slowdowns afterwards in
    figures/machine_drift.csv.

  4. Sensitivity checks (optional, also for the paper)

  caffeinate -i python -m lime_study.run_all --variants ranked_subset
  fixed_kernel

  Each variant is about as long as the main run. The results go to
  results/<variant>/ and figures/<variant>/.

  5. Where the results are

  - Figures: lime_study/figures/, mainly lime_scaling_grid, lime_stability_grid
    and cost_vs_quality.
  - Tables for the paper:
    - figures/paired_differences.csv (model comparisons with Holm-corrected
      p-values)
    - figures/runtime_scaling.csv
    - figures/reference_check.csv
  - Per-model raw data: lime_study/results/.

  To redraw the figures without re-running: python -m lime_study.run_all
  --plots-only.

  Things to avoid

  - Don't use python -m lime_study.models.run_xgboost (or the other single-model
    scripts) for paper results. They run one model on its own, so the results
    can't be compared with the interleaved run, and plotting will refuse to mix
    them.
  - Always run from the repo root with python -m .... Running python
    lime_study/run_all.py directly breaks the imports.
  - If a run stops with a version-mismatch error, reinstall the pins rather than
    using --allow-version-mismatch. That flag is only for non-paper test runs.

⏺ These changes came from Amelia's commit 6e60dc9 on 21 Oct, not from our work
  together, so the explanations are thin. The only write-up is the README's
  "Controls against bias" section plus code comments. Nothing is in the log
  entries or the rebuild plan. Here is what each piece is.

  Why 50 features was added

  The only justification is a code comment in config.py:52
  (lime_study/config.py#L52):

  ▎ "50 adds a fourth design point for the runtime exponent in d and is never
  ▎ underdetermined"

  In plain terms:
  1. For the runtime analysis. The new runtime_scaling.csv fits a curve for how
     runtime grows with the feature count. A fourth point (50, 100, 300, 500)
     makes that fit more reliable than three points would.
  2. There are always more samples than features at 50. Even the smallest budget
     (500 samples) has 10 samples per feature, so every setting at d=50 is
     well-posed. That's useful as a baseline.

  It isn't explained anywhere the team will read, and it contradicts our 20-10
  log entry, which justifies 100 as the "high-dimensional" floor. If 50 stays,
  the log needs a note that it's a low-dimensional baseline and runtime-fit
  point, not one of the high-dimensional conditions.

  What the large-N reference is

  For every case, feature count and model, LIME is also run twice with 50,000
  samples, five times the largest budget, using seeds the normal runs never use.
  These two runs stand in for what LIME's explanation converges to given far
  more samples than anyone would normally use.

  On your question about the graphs: the reference isn't drawn as its own line,
  because it's the target that every other point is measured against. In the
  stability grid (lime_study/figures/quick/lime_stability_grid.png):

  - Row 1, "Top-5 Jaccard vs large-N reference": each normal explanation's top 5
    is compared with each reference's top 5, and the result is averaged over
    both references. A high value means a normal-budget run gets close to the
    answer LIME settles on with lots of samples.
  - Dashed lines, the "reference ceiling": the Jaccard between the two
    references themselves. Even two 50,000-sample runs don't fully agree (about
    0.7–0.9 here). No normal run can be expected to match "the" reference better
    than the references match each other, so the dashed line is the realistic
    maximum.
  - Grey solid line at 0.667: a rule set before the results. The two references
    must share on average at least 4 of their top 5 features to count as a
    trustworthy target. A dotted ceiling means a reference failed that rule, for
    example Logistic Regression and Random Forest at 300 features and Logistic
    Regression at 500. Comparisons against those references aren't treated as
    evidence.
  - Row 2, "Reference agreement / reference ceiling": row 1 divided by the
    ceiling. This corrects for the references themselves being noisier at high
    d, so a drop here means the normal runs really are further from convergence.

  What "top-5 Jaccard between repeats" is

  Row 3 is the stability measure we already had: how much the top 5 features
  overlap between the 5 repeated runs of the same case.

  It differs from the reference measure like this:
  - Repeat Jaccard asks whether runs agree with each other (consistency).
  - Reference Jaccard asks whether runs land near the converged answer
    (convergence).

  Runs can agree with each other at a low budget and still be far from where
  LIME ends up, so the second is the stronger test.

  Held-out surrogate fit and error

  These replace the old fidelity measure.
  - The old measure was explanation.score, LIME's own R². It's computed on the
    same perturbations the surrogate was fitted to, so it's an in-sample score
    and gets inflated at small budgets. It's still saved as fidelity_insample,
    for comparison only.
  - Held-out surrogate fit (R²): for each case and feature count, 5,000 fresh
    perturbations are drawn the same way LIME draws them, but never used for
    fitting. The fitted surrogate predicts them and is scored there. This
    measures fidelity the way a test set measures accuracy.
  - Held-out surrogate error (RMSE): the same test, reported as the average
    prediction error in probability units. It's included because R² breaks down
    when the model's output barely changes near a case (R² divides by that
    change). Those cases are flagged flat_output and left out of R², but they
    still get an RMSE.

  Other things worth raising with Amelia

  - The new figure is unreadable in places: the x-axis labels overlap in the
    stability grid (bottom row).
  - That commit also added files that shouldn't be in git:
    - the 60 MB dataset zip (lime_study/data/, which .gitignore is meant to
      exclude);
    - __pycache__ files.
  - It deleted the old experiments/ folder. That may be intended, but we had
    agreed to leave it untouched.
  - The commit message looks like pasted chat text.

  If you want, I can write a log entry that properly documents these additions:
  the 50-feature condition, the references and ceiling, the held-out metrics,
  and the variants. I can also fix the overlapping axis labels.

⏺ I've read both notes. They're thorough and answer the questions you just
  asked. Below is what they cover, then a few problems I spotted.

  Your earlier questions are answered here

  Your question: Why 50 features?
  Where: Fix 13, and the d-axis table in design_decisions.md
  Short answer: It adds a 4th point for fitting how runtime grows with d (4 × 5
  =
  20 design points). It's never underdetermined (N ≥ 10·d) and costs about 6%
  extra runtime.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Your question: What is the large-N reference?
  Where: Fix 2
  Short answer: Two LIME runs per case × d × model with N = 50,000. They
  estimate
  where LIME converges, so we can measure convergence and not only
  repeatability.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Your question: Reference ceiling and the dotted/grey lines
  Where: Fix 3
  Short answer: The ceiling is the top-5 Jaccard between the two references
  (agreement can't exceed it). The pre-set validity rule is ≥ 4 of 5 shared
  features (0.667).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Your question: Repeat Jaccard vs reference Jaccard
  Where: Fix 2
  Short answer: Repeats measure repeatability: five small-N runs can agree and
  still all be wrong. References measure convergence. Claim "less stable at
  higher d" only if both repeat Jaccard and relative agreement fall.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Your question: Held-out fit/error
  Where: Fixes 1 and 4
  Short answer: explanation.score is in-sample, so small N looked artificially
  good. The old results showed LR fidelity falling from 0.137 to 0.085 as N
  rose. Held-out R² and RMSE on 5,000 fresh perturbations fix this. RMSE is
  added because R² breaks when the model's output is flat.

  Overall the reasoning is sound. In particular, the in-sample fidelity problem
  (fix 1) and the predicted-class mismatch (fix 6) were real flaws in the
  version I built, and I hadn't caught them.

     like real, well-known works, but none have been checked. That's about 27
     references, against the 8 we verified for the dataset. They need the same
     verification pass before the paper.

     design:

       100/300/500 and call 100 the "high-dimensional floor". d = 50 now breaks
       that argument and needs reconciling, for example by calling 50 a
       low-dimensional baseline used for the runtime fit.
     - lime_study_rebuild_plan.md still lists 18 cases, 1,350 explanations and
       explanation.score as fidelity.
     - There's no log entry for 21 Oct explaining any of these changes.
  4. Uncommitted old-design results on your machine. git status shows modified

  ⎿  Error: Exit code 1
     - All sources were verified. See
     `lime_study/notes/dataset_justification.md`.

     ## Why 100, 300 and then 500 features
     - The models always train and predict on **all 561 features**. Only the
     number of features LIME perturbs changes, so model accuracy is the same at
     every step and only explanation dimensionality varies.
     - **Upper bound 500: TabFM's limit.** TabFM's documented `max_num_features`
     is 500. Going above 500 would mean TabFM sees a feature-subsampled input
     while the other models don't, which breaks the controlled comparison. 500
     still covers 89% of the 561 features.
     - **Lower bound 100: the "high-dimensional" floor.** The study is about
     features in the hundreds, so even the smallest condition meets that
     requirement. LIME's 10-feature explanation is then only 10% of the
     perturbed features.
     - **Middle 300: even spacing.** Equal steps of 200 give three equally
     spaced points. Three is the minimum needed to tell a linear trend from a
     curving one, for example stability that drops sharply and then levels off.
     - **Sample-to-feature ratio.** LIME fits a ridge surrogate to `num_samples`
     perturbations over `d` features. Crossed with the 500–10,000 budgets, the
     grid runs from **1:1** (500 samples, 500 features, a barely determined
     surrogate) to **100:1** (10,000 samples, 100 features). LIME's default
     kernel width also grows with dimension: 0.75·√d gives 7.5, 13.0 and 16.8.
     Garreau & von Luxburg (AISTATS 2020) show these parameter choices can make
     LIME miss important features, so these are the regimes where fidelity and
     stability are expected to change.
     - The subsets are **nested** (100 ⊂ 300 ⊂ 500) and chosen with a fixed
     seed, so each step only adds features and the result is reproducible.
     … +11 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

      26  **Why the split is 70/30 rather than our old 80/20.** It is HAR
          's own split, made by volunteer rather than by row. Windows rec
          orded from the same person are highly correlated, so a random r
          ow split would put the same people in both train and test and i
          nflate accuracy. Using the published split also keeps our accur
          acy figures comparable with the original paper (multiclass SVM,
           96%). Covertype's 80/20 was only our own choice, because that 
          dataset had no official split. The ratio is supported for high-
          dimensional classifiers: Dobbin & Simon (source 9) found that a
          bout 2/3 for training is close to optimal when n ≥ 100 and accu
          racy is 85% or higher, and HAR meets both conditions. Splitting
           by subject is supported by Saeb et al. (source 10), who show t
          hat record-wise splits often massively overestimate accuracy.

      24 -## Why 100, 300 and then 500 features                          
      24 +## Why 50, 100, 300 and 500 features                           
      25 +*Updated 21-10-2026: the sweep started as 100 / 300 / 500. d = 
         +50 was added on the `claim-safe-fixes` branch (fix 13 in `lime_
         +study/notes/claim_safe_fixes.md`).*                            
      26 +                                                               
      27  - The models always train and predict on **all 561 features**. 
          Only the number of features LIME perturbs changes, so model acc
          uracy is the same at every step and only explanation dimensiona
          lity varies.
      28  - **Upper bound 500: TabFM's limit.** TabFM's documented `max_n
          um_features` is 500. Going above 500 would mean TabFM sees a fe
          ature-subsampled input while the other models don't, which brea
          ks the controlled comparison. 500 still covers 89% of the 561 f
          eatures.
      27 -- **Lower bound 100: the "high-dimensional" floor.** The study 
         -is about features in the hundreds, so even the smallest conditi
         -on meets that requirement. LIME's 10-feature explanation is the
         -n only 10% of the perturbed features.                          
      28 -- **Middle 300: even spacing.** Equal steps of 200 give three e
         -qually spaced points. Three is the minimum needed to tell a lin
         -ear trend from a curving one, for example stability that drops 
         -sharply and then levels off.                                   
      29 -- **Sample-to-feature ratio.** LIME fits a ridge surrogate to `
         -num_samples` perturbations over `d` features. Crossed with the 
         -500–10,000 budgets, the grid runs from **1:1** (500 samples, 50
         -0 features, a barely determined surrogate) to **100:1** (10,000
         - samples, 100 features). LIME's default kernel width also grows
         - with dimension: 0.75·√d gives 7.5, 13.0 and 16.8. Garreau & vo
         -n Luxburg (AISTATS 2020) show these parameter choices can make 
         -LIME miss important features, so these are the regimes where fi
         -delity and stability are expected to change.                   
      30 -- The subsets are **nested** (100 ⊂ 300 ⊂ 500) and chosen with 
         -a fixed seed, so each step only adds features and the result is
         - reproducible.                                                 
      31 -- **Caveat:** no source prescribes exactly 100/300/500. The two
         - ends are justified (TabFM's limit and the "hundreds" floor), b
         -ut 300 is a design choice for even spacing. Present it in the p
         -aper as a design decision.                                     
      29 +- **100, 300, 500: the high-dimensional conditions.** The study
         + is about features in the hundreds. These three are equally spa
         +ced (steps of 200), and they are the conditions the paper's "hi
         +gh-dimensional" claims rest on.                                
      30 +- **50: a low-dimensional baseline below the "hundreds" range, 
         +on purpose.** It is not a high-dimensional condition, and it is
         + there for three reasons:                                      
      31 +  - **Runtime scaling needs a fourth point.** The runtime analy
         +sis fits a power law (log runtime = a + b·log N + c·log d, Gold
         +smith et al. 2007). With only three d values there are almost n
         +o degrees of freedom left to check whether the N exponent chang
         +es with d. Four d values × five budgets gives 20 design points.
      32 +  - **It shows where degradation starts.** Without a low-dimens
         +ional reference, the study could only show LIME at 100–500 feat
         +ures. With d = 50, the paper can show quality falling *from* a 
         +setting where LIME should work well, not just that it is poor e
         +verywhere in the high-dimensional range.                       
      33 +  - **It is always well-determined.** Even the smallest budget 
         +gives 10 samples per feature (500 / 50), so no d = 50 setting i
         +s underdetermined.                                             
      34 +- **Why 50 specifically:** it halves 100, so 50 → 100 is the sa
         +me proportional step as a doubling. It is still 5× the 10 featu
         +res LIME reports, so the explanation has to choose features rat
         +her than list almost everything. It is also the cheapest condit
         +ion, adding an estimated ~6% to the run time.                  
      35 +- **Sample-to-feature ratio.** LIME fits a ridge surrogate to `
         +num_samples` perturbations over `d` features. Crossed with the 
         +500–10,000 budgets, the grid now runs from **1:1** (500 samples
         +, 500 features, a barely determined surrogate, flagged `underde
         +termined`) to **200:1** (10,000 samples, 50 features). LIME's d
         +efault kernel width also grows with dimension: 0.75·√d gives 5.
         +3, 7.5, 13.0 and 16.8. Garreau & von Luxburg (AISTATS 2020) sho
         +w these parameter choices can make LIME miss important features
         +, so this range covers where fidelity and stability are expecte
         +d to change.                                                   
      36 +- The subsets are **nested** (50 ⊂ 100 ⊂ 300 ⊂ 500) and chosen 
         +with a fixed seed, so each step only adds features and the resu
         +lt is reproducible.                                            
      37 +- **For the paper:** report d = 50 as a low-dimensional baselin
         +e and runtime-fit point, and keep "high-dimensional" claims to 
         +100–500.                                                       
      38 +- **Caveat:** no source prescribes these exact values. 500 is f
         +ixed by TabFM, the 100–500 range by the "hundreds" goal, and 50
         + by the reasons above. The exact spacing is a design decision.

  Overlapping labels: fixed

  The cause was that each of the four columns repeated a long x-axis label
  ("LIME samples (num_samples)") in a panel only about 1.6 inches wide, so
  neighbouring labels ran into each other. In plot_results.py
  (lime_study/plot_results.py), each figure now has one shared label centred
  under all the panels, with the footnote below it so its wrapped lines can't
  collide with the label. I redrew the quick figures and checked all three:
  lime_stability_grid, lime_scaling_grid and cost_vs_quality. The full-run
  figures will pick this up automatically.

  d = 50 justification: added to the older docs

  The branch's own reason was only the runtime-fit argument. I added my own
  reasoning so that d = 50 no longer contradicts the "hundreds of features"
  floor. It's framed as a deliberate low-dimensional baseline, not a
  high-dimensional condition:

  1. Runtime scaling needs a fourth point. This is the branch's reason: 4 × 5 =
     20 design points for the power-law fit (Goldsmith et al. 2007).
  2. It shows where degradation starts. Without it, the paper could only show
     LIME at 100–500. With it, quality can be seen falling from a setting where
     LIME should work well.
  3. It's always well-determined. Every setting has at least 10 samples per
     feature.
  4. Why 50 specifically: it halves 100, it's still 5× the 10 features LIME
     reports, and it's the cheapest condition (about 6% extra run time).

  The updated numbers are a sample-to-feature range of 1:1 to 200:1, and kernel
  widths of 5.3, 7.5, 13.0 and 16.8. Every doc also carries the same rule for
  the paper: keep "high-dimensional" claims to 100–500 and describe 50 as the
  baseline. No source prescribes these exact values, so that caveat is stated
  too.

❯ Looking at the graphs there are the points and lines, which represent each
  model. but there are also individual points that are using the same shape of
  the points, but hollowed out, and i only see it in the 500 pertubations
  graph.

⏺ Those hollow markers are deliberate. They mark the underdetermined setting: a
  sample budget no larger than the number of perturbed features (N ≤ d). In this
  sweep that only happens in one place, 500 samples with 500 features, which is
  why you only see them in the 500-feature panels.

  Why that setting is treated differently
  - With 10 features to report, LIME first fits a ridge regression on all d
    perturbed features to choose them. With 500 samples and 500 features there
    are as many unknowns as data points, so the chosen features are mostly
    noise.
  - You can see it in the results: at that setting, top-5 Jaccard between
    repeats is about 0.01–0.03, which is essentially random.

  Why it's hollow and not joined to the line
  - Connecting it would drag every 500-feature trend line down at the left end,
    and the curve would partly reflect a broken setting rather than how LIME
    scales.
  - It's still drawn rather than deleted, so readers can see what happens when N
    ≤ d.
  - It's also excluded from the trend fits and from the primary statistical
    tests (fix 5 in claim_safe_fixes.md).

  Why the 500-feature panel looks odd in the figures you're viewing
  Those are the quick smoke-test figures, which only have two budgets (500 and
  1,000). Once the hollow N = 500 point is set aside, only one filled point per
  model is left at N = 1,000, so there's nothing to draw a line between. In the
  full run that panel will show a normal line from 1k to 10k, plus the hollow
  point at 500.

  The only explanation on the figure itself is the footnote: "Hollow markers: N
  ≤ d, kept out of the lines." That's easy to miss. If you want, I can add a
  hollow-marker entry to the legend, such as "○ N ≤ d (underdetermined)", so
  it's explained where readers look first.

❯ is it of significance to the graph?

⏺ It matters for one specific point, but not for the main results.

  It doesn't drive the main results
  - It's one point per model, in one panel.
  - It's kept out of the trend lines, the runtime scaling fit and the primary
    statistical tests. Deleting it would change none of the paper's main claims
    or numbers.

  It does show something worth reporting
  - It's the clearest example of LIME breaking down. When the sample budget
    doesn't exceed the number of perturbed features, the explanations become
    essentially random (repeat Jaccard about 0.01–0.03).
  - That gives a practical guideline for readers: the sample budget must clearly
    exceed the number of features being perturbed. LIME's default of 5,000
    samples is fine at 500 features, but anyone who lowers it to save time on
    wide data can fall into this trap.
  - It also shows the effect is sharp. At 500 features, going from 500 to 1,000
    samples lifts stability from near zero to a usable level.

  When you'd drop it
  If the paper doesn't make the "budget must exceed d" point, the hollow markers
  just add visual clutter that needs a footnote. You could then remove them
  from the figure and mention the result in one sentence, or put it in a table.

  My recommendation: keep it, and use it as one of the paper's practical
  takeaways. It's an easy, concrete guideline that follows directly from the
  data. If you keep it, adding a hollow-marker entry to the legend (e.g. "○ N ≤
  d (underdetermined)") would make it self-explanatory instead of relying on the
  footnote. That's a decision for whoever writes the results section (Hugh)
  together with the team.

  Review of edc122c (fixes 19–24)

  Overall this is solid work, and the reasoning is documented in
  claim_safe_fixes.md.

  - Fix 19, cases spread across the 9 test participants, plus
    participant-clustered CIs. This is a real issue I missed. Windows from the
    same person are correlated, so treating 30 cases as fully independent
    overstates confidence. The new selection caps each participant at 4 cases,
    and the extra _cluster intervals resample participants. I checked that the
    bootstrap weights line up with the cases (both are ordered by case_index).
    With only 9 participants those intervals are approximate, which the note
    says.
  - Fix 23, "underdetermined" renamed to "low sample-to-feature ratio." This is
    correct: LIME's feature-selection ridge always has a unique solution, so N ≤
    d isn't strictly underdetermined. The cutoff and exclusions are unchanged.
    My stashed d = 50 text still says "underdetermined", so I'd update that
    wording when restoring it.
  - Fix 21, RMSE kept in the primary family but labelled "absolute," with the
    model's local output spread alongside. This is reasonable. Swapping a
    primary metric after seeing results would be outcome-driven. Their rule is
    worth keeping: a lower RMSE doesn't mean a better explanation unless R² is
    also no worse.
  - Fix 22, speed-probe warm-up. This is fine, but the finding behind it
    matters: machine speed varied by up to about 50% within a run. The
    randomised block design protects the model comparisons, but runtimes can't
    be compared across separate runs (for example main vs fixed_kernel), and the
    full run needs a quiet machine on power.
  - Fix 24, run_all runs all variants before plotting. This is a correct fix.

  Still unresolved

  - .gitignore is now committed, but the junk is still tracked: the 58 MB data
    zip and the __pycache__ files need git rm --cached.
  - requirements.txt and tabfm/ now show as untracked, because the new
    .gitignore no longer covers them. tabfm/ is a separate git repo, so a
    careless git add . would pull it in. Both should be added to .gitignore.
  - The references cited in claim_safe_fixes.md are still unverified (cited from
    memory).

  │ Large-N references: 720 runs at 50,000 samples (3       │ 27.1  │ 39%   │
  │ models × 30 cases × 4 feature counts × 2)               │ min   │       │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼───────┤
  │ Everything else: held-out scoring, building a LIME      │ ~14   │       │
  │ explainer for every run, warm-ups, speed probes, model  │ min   │ 21%   │
  │ fitting, analysis                                       │       │       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴───────┴───────┘

  The changes responsible, biggest first

  1. Two sensitivity variants (ranked_subset, fixed_kernel): about ×3. Each is a
     complete re-run of the study, so they add about 2 × 70 minutes. This is
     the largest single factor.
  2. Large-N references: about +27 minutes per run. Each is LIME with 50,000
     samples, five times the largest budget. 720 of them cost almost as much as
     all 9,000 normal explanations together.
  3. More explained cases (18 → 30): about ×1.67 on everything done per case:
     explanations, references and held-out scoring.
  4. The d = 50 condition: small. It's the cheapest feature count (31–59 seconds
     per model). The 500-feature settings are the expensive ones.
  5. Held-out fidelity: part of the ~14 minutes. Every explanation is now scored
     on 5,000 fresh perturbations, and the model has to predict those once per
     case and feature count.

  If you need it to be shorter

  - Run main first (about 70 minutes) and the two variants separately, for
    example overnight. The variants are robustness checks, so the main results
    don't depend on them.
  - Smaller references (e.g. 20,000 samples instead of 50,000) would save about
    15 minutes per run. But the references would agree with each other less, and
    that agreement is what the pre-set 0.667 validity rule measures. 50,000 was
    fixed before seeing results, so changing it now is hard to justify.
  - Fewer cases or repeats would cut time in proportion, but would undo the
    reason they were increased (more reliable confidence intervals).

  My recommendation is to keep the design and split the runs: main when you can
  leave the Mac alone for an hour, then --variants ranked_subset fixed_kernel
  overnight. Runtimes aren't compared across separate runs anyway, so running
  them at different times costs nothing.

❯ does the code answer the following:
  This is a controlled computational experiment characterizing how LIME’s cost
  and quality scale with the number of samples (N) and features (d) using a
  factorial design (3 models x 4 values of d x 5 values of N x 5 repeats).

  The three research questions this experiment aims to address are:

  How does runtime scale with N and d per explanation?

  How does quality change with N and d?

  How does the black-box model change cost/quality trade-offs?

  The unit of analysis is 30 explained instances – 5 instances per class,
  controlled and evaluated via ranked subsets and a fixed kernel width at
  0.75·√300 to prevent differences in computation per instance run.

⏺ Mostly yes. The design and the three research questions match the code. The
  last sentence does not: it describes the sensitivity checks as if they were
  the main design, and gives them the wrong purpose.

  What matches the code

  Claim: 3 models × 4 d × 5 N × 5 repeats
  Code: XGBoost / Logistic Regression / Random Forest; d = 50, 100, 300, 500; N
  =
  500, 1k, 2.5k, 5k, 10k; 5 repeats (config.py (lime_study/config.py))
  Verdict: ✓ Strictly, the repeats are replicates, not a factor, so "3 × 4 × 5
  factorial with 5 repeats" is more precise.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Claim: RQ1: runtime scaling with N and d
  Code: runtime_scaling.csv fits log runtime = a + b·log N + c·log d per model,
  giving scaling exponents, plus within-d/within-N slopes and an interaction
  term
  Verdict: ✓ Answered directly
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Claim: RQ2: quality vs N and d
  Code: Held-out R² and RMSE (fidelity), Jaccard against the large-N references
  (convergence) and repeat Jaccard (stability), per d × N
  Verdict: ✓ Answered. Flat cases are left out of R², and N ≤ d is shown
  separately.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Claim: RQ3: effect of the black-box model
  Code: paired_differences.csv gives case-paired model comparisons, with a
  Holm-corrected primary family at N = 5,000. runtime_scaling.csv also compares
   the models' exponents.
  Verdict: ✓ Answered, but only for these exact (untuned) model settings, not
  model families in general
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Claim: 30 explained instances, 5 per class
  Code: FULL_SWEEP: 5 per class × 6 classes
  Verdict: ✓

  What's wrong: the last sentence

  ▎ "controlled and evaluated via ranked subsets and a fixed kernel width at
  ▎ 0.75·√300 to prevent differences in computation per instance run"

  1. These aren't the main design. The main study (main variant) uses random
     nested feature subsets and LIME's default kernel width, 0.75·√d, which
     changes with d. ranked_subset and fixed_kernel are two separate sensitivity
     runs.
  2. Their purpose is different. They don't equalise computation. They test
     whether the d-trends are caused by something other than d:
     - ranked_subset checks that trends aren't caused by random subsets
       happening to contain more or less useful signal.
     - fixed_kernel checks that trends aren't caused by LIME's neighbourhood
       size growing with d.

     A trend that holds in all three runs is attributable to d itself.
  3. Equal computation per instance is controlled by other things:
     - every model explains the same 30 rows and the same class;
     - each case gets the same random seeds across models, d and N;
     - every model uses the same nested feature subsets;
     - single-threaded execution;
     - all models run interleaved in a randomised block design, so machine
       slowdowns affect them equally;
     - warm-ups, and garbage collection kept out of the timing.

  Two details worth adding

  - The 30 instances come from only 9 test participants (at most 4 cases each).
    The code reports participant-clustered intervals as a conservative check.
  - The main model comparisons are made at N = 5,000 (LIME's default), chosen in
    advance.

  Suggested rewrite of the last sentence

  ▎ The unit of analysis is the explained instance: 30 held-out rows, 5 per
  ▎ class, drawn from the 9 test participants with at most 4 per participant.
  ▎ Every model explains the same rows and class with the same per-instance
  ▎ random seeds, and all models run interleaved in a randomized block design,
  ▎ so runtime and quality differences are not caused by different inputs,
  ▎ randomness or machine state. Two sensitivity runs check that d-trends are
  ▎ not artefacts of the design: one replaces random feature subsets with
  ▎ subsets ranked by ANOVA F (ranked_subset), and one fixes LIME's kernel width
  ▎ at 0.75·√300 ≈ 13.0 for every d (fixed_kernel).

  pandas Series, which matplotlib coerces with float() — something that will
  become a hard error in a future pandas version. I'll update the helper to
  return plain arrays instead.

  three rows, each plotted against the sample budget, with a column for each
  feature count (50, 100, 300, 500):

  1. Runtime per explanation.
  2. Fidelity: held-out surrogate fit (weighted R²).
  3. Stability: top-5 Jaccard between repeated runs.

  It uses the same style as the other figures: 95% confidence bands, hollow
  markers for N ≤ d, and the shared x-axis label. Its footnote points readers to
  the two detailed grids, lime_scaling_grid (which adds RMSE) and
  lime_stability_grid (which adds the large-N references). I also added it to
  the README's output list.

  Testing it:
  - I re-ran the quick study for main and fixed_kernel. The quick results on
    disk were still from before Amelia's fix 19, so the plots couldn't run until
    they were regenerated. They're now in the current format.
  - The quick overview is at lime_study/figures/quick/lime_overview.png. It only
    shows 2 budgets, because that's all the smoke test runs.
  - The full run will produce lime_study/figures/lime_overview.png, plus one in
    each variant's folder.

  Fixed along the way: the dozens of FutureWarnings printed during plotting.
  They came from the error bars on the single hollow N ≤ d point, where pandas
  Series were passed to matplotlib. The next pandas version will turn that
  warning into an error, so it would eventually have broken plotting. The helper
  now passes plain arrays, and both variants plot with zero warnings.

  Nothing is committed yet, and the full run is still the remaining step before
  there are paper-ready figures.
